Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.

It’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.

I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.

It’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.

required\n  manifestation: My family has had to step in with bills and paperwork, and people at church and the garden have had to redo plans or cover responsibilities I dropped. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.\n\nIt’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.

I mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.

I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I interrupt people more than I mean to, especially in committee meetings, because a thought pops into my head and I blurt it out before I lose it, even when someone else is still talking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.

So I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.

I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.\n\nIt’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.

And it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.

I feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.

That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.

I don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.

It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s been especially bad since my husband died… that was about a year and ten months ago now. I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.

Since then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.

It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of restlessness and inability to settle?\n\nPatient: It’s been especially bad since my husband died… that was about a year and ten months ago now. I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.\n\nSince then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F41.1", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I feel embarrassed because groups I care about no longer feel they can count on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have. I don’t know if it’s only the restlessness or just everything getting harder to manage, but my relationships have definitely changed because of it.

My family has had to step in more than I would like to admit, especially with bills and paperwork. I’ll let things sit, or I’ll think I’m going to take care of them and then somehow it gets away from me, and then someone has to help sort it out. That’s very embarrassing. I’m 66, and I don’t want my children or relatives feeling like they have to check up on me like that.

And socially, yes… at church and in the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through, and then other people had to redo plans or cover what I didn’t do. I feel awful about that because those are people and groups I care about. It’s not just missing one little thing. It feels like people have started to think, “Well, we can’t really count on her,” and that hurts. I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.

---------- turn 10 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.\n\nIt’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of restlessness and inability to settle?\n\nPatient: It’s been especially bad since my husband died… that was about a year and ten months ago now. I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.\n\nSince then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your social life or relationships since you started experiencing this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I don’t know if it’s only the restlessness or just everything getting harder to manage, but my relationships have definitely changed because of it.\n\nMy family has had to step in more than I would like to admit, especially with bills and paperwork. I’ll let things sit, or I’ll think I’m going to take care of them and then somehow it gets away from me, and then someone has to help sort it out. That’s very embarrassing. I’m 66, and I don’t want my children or relatives feeling like they have to check up on me like that.\n\nAnd socially, yes… at church and in the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through, and then other people had to redo plans or cover what I didn’t do. I feel awful about that because those are people and groups I care about. It’s not just missing one little thing. It feels like people have started to think, “Well, we can’t really count on her,” and that hurts. I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F41.1", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your restlessness is usually triggered by feeling anxious or worried about something specific?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of restlessness and inability to settle?\n\nPatient: It’s been especially bad since my husband died… that was about a year and ten months ago now. I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.\n\nSince then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your social life or relationships since you started experiencing this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I don’t know if it’s only the restlessness or just everything getting harder to manage, but my relationships have definitely changed because of it.\n\nMy family has had to step in more than I would like to admit, especially with bills and paperwork. I’ll let things sit, or I’ll think I’m going to take care of them and then somehow it gets away from me, and then someone has to help sort it out. That’s very embarrassing. I’m 66, and I don’t want my children or relatives feeling like they have to check up on me like that.\n\nAnd socially, yes… at church and in the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through, and then other people had to redo plans or cover what I didn’t do. I feel awful about that because those are people and groups I care about. It’s not just missing one little thing. It feels like people have started to think, “Well, we can’t really count on her,” and that hurts. I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your restlessness is usually triggered by feeling anxious or worried about something specific?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 66 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I feel like I cannot settle inside my own skin anymore, like I have to jump up and do the next thing even when I am worn out and should be sitting still.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sometimes there’s a worry attached to it, yes, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only when I’m anxious about one specific thing. That’s part of why I came in, honestly. It feels more like I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.

I’ll be sitting there, and even if I’m exhausted and I know I should rest, there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Not always because I’ve thought of something urgent, either. It’s more like my body or my mind won’t let me be still. I’ll think, “Just sit down for a minute,” and then I’m up again, moving toward something else, or feeling like I should be.

So, I guess worry can make it worse, but it’s not always triggered by a clear worry. It’s just this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite get comfortable being still.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.\n\nIt’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me.   {

I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.\n\nSince then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your social life or relationships since you started experiencing this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I don’t know if it’s only the restlessness or just everything getting harder to manage, but my relationships have definitely changed because of it.\n\nMy family has had to step in more than I would like to admit, especially with bills and paperwork. I’ll let things sit, or I’ll think I’m going to take care of them and then somehow it gets away from me, and then someone has to help sort it out. That’s very embarrassing. I’m 66, and I don’t want my children or relatives feeling like they have to check up on me like that.\n\nAnd socially, yes… at church and in the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through, and then other people had to redo plans or cover what I didn’t do. I feel awful about that because those are people and groups I care about. It’s not just missing one little thing. It feels like people have started to think, “Well, we can’t really count on her,” and that hurts. I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is usually triggered by feeling anxious or worried about something specific?\n\nPatient: Sometimes there’s a worry attached to it, yes, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only when I’m anxious about one specific thing. That’s part of why I came in, honestly. It feels more like I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and even if I’m exhausted and I know I should rest, there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Not always because I’ve thought of something urgent, either. It’s more like my body or my mind won’t let me be still. I’ll think, “Just sit down for a minute,” and then I’m up again, moving toward something else, or feeling like I should be.\n\nSo, I guess worry can make it worse, but it’s not always triggered by a clear worry. It’s just this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite get comfortable being still.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced significant difficulties with organization or time management throughout your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just something that started recently. Even when I was little, in grade school, I remember the teachers saying I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot, “careless,” and it still bothers me a bit because I don’t think I was trying not to care.

I was always losing papers, forgetting where I put things, not having what I needed when I needed it. And they’d say I talked out of turn or needed reminders to sit still and finish my work. I can remember being told, “Stay in your seat,” or “Finish what you started,” more than once. At the time I guess people just thought I was scattered or not applying myself, but when you ask it that way, yes, those kinds of problems have been there for a very long time.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came in because I just feel like I can’t settle inside myself anymore. It’s like I’m sitting there, and I know I should be resting, I know I’m worn out, but my body—or maybe my mind, I don’t even know—keeps pushing me like I need to get up and do the next thing.\n\nIt’s not just ordinary “I’m busy” feeling. It feels like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin, like I can’t just be still. I’ll try to sit, and there’s this pressure inside, like I have to jump up, move, start something, go do something else, even when there’s no good reason and I’m tired. And it’s gotten to the point where it’s really bothering me, because I feel like I should be able to calm down and I just… can’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Recurring thoughts… I’m not sure I’d call it that exactly. I mean, I do get things going around in my head sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t know that there’s one particular thought that keeps coming back and I can’t shake it.\n\nIt’s more like my mind doesn’t always feel quiet, if that makes sense, but not in the way of one specific idea or fear repeating over and over. I’m trying to think if there’s an example, but nothing clear is coming to me. So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed recurring thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “on edge” is a good way to put it, though it feels more physical than worry, if that makes sense. It’s like I can’t settle inside myself. I’ll be sitting there, and I know I should just stay put, I know I’m worn out and need to rest, but there’s this pressure in me like I have to get up, like I need to jump to the next thing.\n\nIt’s not always that I’m scared of something happening. It’s more that I feel keyed up and restless, like my body won’t let me be still. Even when there’s no real reason to move or do anything, I still feel pushed to do it. That’s what’s been bothering me so much — I feel like I should be able to sit quietly, and I just can’t seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having a lot of thoughts racing through your mind when you're feeling this restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I don’t know that I’d describe it as “racing thoughts,” exactly. When I feel that restlessness, it’s more like this internal push or pressure to move or do something, rather than a whole stream of thoughts flying through my mind.\n\nI mean, I might think, “I should get up,” or “maybe I should do this other thing,” but it doesn’t feel like my thoughts are speeding so fast I can’t catch them. It’s more my body feels unsettled, like I can’t just sit and be comfortable. So… no, I haven’t really noticed a lot of thoughts racing. It’s more the feeling of not being able to stay still.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though for me the clearest example is talking. I interrupt people more than I mean to. It happens especially in committee meetings, where someone will be in the middle of making a point, and a thought pops into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, I’m going to lose it.\n\nSo I’ll just blurt it out, and then I realize, oh, they weren’t finished. I don’t mean to be rude, and I’m not trying to take over, but it comes out before I’ve really thought through how it’s going to sound or whether it’s the right time. Sometimes afterward I feel embarrassed, because I can see that I jumped in too quickly. It’s like there’s a very small space between the thought and me saying it, and I don’t always catch myself in time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I do, at least not in the way you might mean. When you say repeat actions, I think of things like having to check a lock over and over, or washing hands again and again, or doing something a certain number of times, and I don’t really recognize that in myself.\n\nI might go back to something if I’ve forgotten whether I did it, or I might restart a task because I got distracted, but it doesn’t feel like I’m driven to repeat it in a ritual kind of way. It’s more that I lose track sometimes and then have to re-orient myself. But no, I haven’t noticed a need to repeat certain behaviors over and over just to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing problems in your work, relationships, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wish I could say it was just annoying, but it has caused real problems. I’m not working now, but even in my regular life, my family has had to step in with bills and paperwork because I let things pile up or I don’t keep track of what needs to be done. That’s embarrassing at my age, honestly. I don’t like feeling like my children or relatives have to sort of supervise me with ordinary adult things.\n\nAnd it’s affected things outside the house too. At church, and with the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through the way I was supposed to, and then other people have had to change plans, redo things, or cover for me. I care about those groups, so that part really bothers me. It’s not just that I forgot one small thing and everyone moved on. It feels like people have started to assume they can’t count on me the way they used to.\n\nI feel ashamed saying that, because I want to be helpful and reliable. I don’t want to be the person other people have to work around. But yes, it’s definitely been causing problems in my daily life and in my relationships with people I care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or images that keep coming into your mind that you try to ignore or suppress?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I have worries that come up, like anyone does, and sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something I forgot to do or whether I handled something badly. But when you say thoughts or images that keep coming into my mind that I’m trying to suppress, I think of something more intrusive, like it pops in against my will and I have to fight it off, and I don’t think that’s really me.\n\nI don’t have disturbing pictures in my mind that repeat over and over, or thoughts that feel frightening in that way. If I’m bothered by a thought, it’s usually because I’m concerned about something real that I need to deal with, not because it feels like my mind is forcing some unwanted image on me. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of restlessness and inability to settle?\n\nPatient: It’s been especially bad since my husband died… that was about a year and ten months ago now. I mean, I may have always been a little fidgety or had trouble sitting still, but the kind that’s been causing real trouble, where I just can’t seem to settle myself or get through things calmly, that really started after he passed.\n\nSince then it’s felt much harder to quiet myself down. I keep thinking, “I should be able to just sit and do what needs doing,” but I don’t feel settled the way I used to. So I’d say almost two years, really — about a year and ten months.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your social life or relationships since you started experiencing this restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I don’t know if it’s only the restlessness or just everything getting harder to manage, but my relationships have definitely changed because of it.\n\nMy family has had to step in more than I would like to admit, especially with bills and paperwork. I’ll let things sit, or I’ll think I’m going to take care of them and then somehow it gets away from me, and then someone has to help sort it out. That’s very embarrassing. I’m 66, and I don’t want my children or relatives feeling like they have to check up on me like that.\n\nAnd socially, yes… at church and in the garden group, I’ve dropped responsibilities or not followed through, and then other people had to redo plans or cover what I didn’t do. I feel awful about that because those are people and groups I care about. It’s not just missing one little thing. It feels like people have started to think, “Well, we can’t really count on her,” and that hurts. I used to want to be someone helpful, someone dependable, and now I feel like I’m making more work for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is usually triggered by feeling anxious or worried about something specific?\n\nPatient: Sometimes there’s a worry attached to it, yes, but it doesn’t feel like it’s only when I’m anxious about one specific thing. That’s part of why I came in, honestly. It feels more like I just cannot settle inside my own skin anymore.\n\nI’ll be sitting there, and even if I’m exhausted and I know I should rest, there’s this feeling like I have to jump up and do the next thing. Not always because I’ve thought of something urgent, either. It’s more like my body or my mind won’t let me be still. I’ll think, “Just sit down for a minute,” and then I’m up again, moving toward something else, or feeling like I should be.\n\nSo, I guess worry can make it worse, but it’s not always triggered by a clear worry. It’s just this unsettled feeling, like I can’t quite get comfortable being still.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with organization or time management throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t just something that started recently. Even when I was little, in grade school, I remember the teachers saying I was bright, but careless. That word came up a lot, “careless,” and it still bothers me a bit because I don’t think I was trying not to care.\n\nI was always losing papers, forgetting where I put things, not having what I needed when I needed it. And they’d say I talked out of turn or needed reminders to sit still and finish my work. I can remember being told, “Stay in your seat,” or “Finish what you started,” more than once.
